I’m not sure… like, “a lot” is hard for me to say. I don’t think I sit down and eat, like, a whole bunch all at once the way people talk about it.

Sometimes if I’m really tired after the warehouse, I’ll pick at stuff or eat more than I meant to, but then I start thinking about it and get kind of uncomfortable. But I don’t know if that counts. And then I might just try to be careful later, or keep busy, but it’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not something I have a clear name for.

I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I can pick out a time like that exactly. Like sometimes after work, if I haven’t really eaten much, I’ll get home and kind of stand in the kitchen and take bites of random things, and then I’m like, “Why did I do that?” But I don’t know if it’s a lot compared to other people.

So… I don’t know. More than once, maybe feeling bothered after eating, but I’m not sure about “a lot in a short time.”

I mean, I don’t really see it like that all the time, but most days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class.\n\nIt’s just, sitting down for a real meal feels like it messes up everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know? Like my whole day gets thrown off. And the warehouse schedule is already a lot—packing stuff all day, people rushing around—so I just kind of keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not eating much or skipping meals, is it because you’re worried about gaining weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s part of it. Like, if I eat something heavy, like fried food from the break room, or if I’m at church and someone gives me a big plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away.\n\nAnd then it kind of sticks in my head. Like I’ll be trying to do other stuff, folding laundry or thinking about class, and I’m still wondering if I need to check the scale. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But yeah, I do get scared about it.\n\nDoctor: After you eat something that worries you about gaining weight, do you ever try to compensate by doing things like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Not really sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes after I eat something that feels like too much, I get really antsy and I don’t want to just sit there, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt work I’m already moving around a lot anyway, packing boxes and walking back and forth, and sometimes I tell myself that counts. But, um… I don’t know about the other stuff. I don’t really think of it like a plan or anything. Like my sister can eat a normal plate and just go watch TV, and I’m still thinking about what was on the plate. I don’t… I can’t think of a time where I’d say definitely, like more than most people. That word makes me nervous because I don’t really know what most people eat.

I mean, I’ve had times where I eat something and then keep grabbing little things, like crackers or whatever’s around, and then I feel weird about it, but it’s not like I finished some huge meal. At work people bring snacks sometimes, and everyone just eats them standing around the break area, and I never know if what I’m doing looks normal or not. One guy eats like three sandwiches and nobody says anything, so then I’m like, okay, maybe I don’t understand portions.

So… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a time like that. I mostly just remember feeling bad after, more than remembering that it was actually a lot.

I guess… maybe? I’m not totally sure what out of control means there. Like I don’t think I’m blacking out or anything, but sometimes I’ll start eating something and then my head gets loud about it, and I feel like I need to stop, or like I shouldn’t have started. 

But that might just be me being weird about food, not losing control. Like if there’s crackers on the counter, I might have a few and then keep thinking about whether I had too many, and then I’m also thinking about whether my sister left the bag open because she always does that and then they get stale, and it makes me mad because I’m the one who ends up cleaning up the kitchen.

So… I don’t know. I’ve felt upset or kind of panicky while eating, but I don’t know if I’d call it out of control.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I can say “definitely,” no. Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.

Sometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.

So… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.

And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.

That’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. So I don’t know if that counts as balancing it out, but yeah, I do avoid eating more after sometimes.

Like my sister can eat a normal plate and just go watch TV, and I’m still thinking about what was on the plate. She also leaves dishes everywhere, which drives me crazy, because then I end up cleaning after her, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nSo… I don’t know. More than once, maybe feeling bothered after eating, but I’m not sure about “a lot in a short time.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a definitely larger amount of food than most people would in a similar situation, like finishing a whole big meal or a lot of snacks in a short time, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I can’t think of a time where I’d say definitely, like more than most people. That word makes me nervous because I don’t really know what most people eat.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I eat something and then keep grabbing little things, like crackers or whatever’s around, and then I feel weird about it, but it’s not like I finished some huge meal. At work people bring snacks sometimes, and everyone just eats them standing around the break area, and I never know if what I’m doing looks normal or not. One guy eats like three sandwiches and nobody says anything, so then I’m like, okay, maybe I don’t understand portions.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a time like that. I mostly just remember feeling bad after, more than remembering that it was actually a lot.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe? I’m not totally sure what out of control means there. Like I don’t think I’m blacking out or anything, but sometimes I’ll start eating something and then my head gets loud about it, and I feel like I need to stop, or like I shouldn’t have started. \n\nBut that might just be me being weird about food, not losing control. Like if there’s crackers on the counter, I might have a few and then keep thinking about whether I had too many, and then I’m also thinking about whether my sister left the bag open because she always does that and then they get stale, and it makes me mad because I’m the one who ends up cleaning up the kitchen.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve felt upset or kind of panicky while eating, but I don’t know if I’d call it out of control.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “definitely,” no. Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.\n\nSometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve tried to make up for eating even a small or normal amount of food by not eating for the rest of the day, exercising more than usual, or doing something else to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.\n\nThat’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. Yeah… I think it does. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.

Like before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. And then it’s like that decides the whole day, almost. If I think I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, like I can handle things. But if I think I look bad, it feels like I already messed up somehow.

I mean, I don’t really see it like that all the time, but most days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class.\n\nIt’s just, sitting down for a real meal feels like it messes up everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know? Like my whole day gets thrown off. And the warehouse schedule is already a lot—packing stuff all day, people rushing around—so I just kind of keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not eating much or skipping meals, is it because you’re worried about gaining weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s part of it. Like, if I eat something heavy, like fried food from the break room, or if I’m at church and someone gives me a big plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away.\n\nAnd then it kind of sticks in my head. Like I’ll be trying to do other stuff, folding laundry or thinking about class, and I’m still wondering if I need to check the scale. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But yeah, I do get scared about it.\n\nDoctor: After you eat something that worries you about gaining weight, do you ever try to compensate by doing things like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Not really sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes after I eat something that feels like too much, I get really antsy and I don’t want to just sit there, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt work I’m already moving around a lot anyway, packing boxes and walking back and forth, and sometimes I tell myself that counts. But, um… I don’t know about the other stuff. I don’t really think of it like a plan or anything. It’s more like I just feel uncomfortable and then I try to get through the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself eating a lot in a short amount of time, more than most people would in similar circumstances, and then felt like you needed to do something to make up for it, like overexercising or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… like, “a lot” is hard for me to say. I don’t think I sit down and eat, like, a whole bunch all at once the way people talk about it.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired after the warehouse, I’ll pick at stuff or eat more than I meant to, but then I start thinking about it and get kind of uncomfortable. But I don’t know if that counts. And then I might just try to be careful later, or keep busy, but it’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not something I have a clear name for.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering it right. I just get mixed up when it’s worded like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a lot more than usual in a short amount of time, maybe even to the point of feeling uncomfortable? Did that happen more than once?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I can pick out a time like that exactly. Like sometimes after work, if I haven’t really eaten much, I’ll get home and kind of stand in the kitchen and take bites of random things, and then I’m like, “Why did I do that?” But I don’t know if it’s a lot compared to other people.\n\nFeeling uncomfortable, yeah, but sometimes that’s just because I’m thinking about it too much, not because I’m actually full, if that makes sense. Like my sister can eat a normal plate and just go watch TV, and I’m still thinking about what was on the plate. She also leaves dishes everywhere, which drives me crazy, because then I end up cleaning after her, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nSo… I don’t know. More than once, maybe feeling bothered after eating, but I’m not sure about “a lot in a short time.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a definitely larger amount of food than most people would in a similar situation, like finishing a whole big meal or a lot of snacks in a short time, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I can’t think of a time where I’d say definitely, like more than most people. That word makes me nervous because I don’t really know what most people eat.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I eat something and then keep grabbing little things, like crackers or whatever’s around, and then I feel weird about it, but it’s not like I finished some huge meal. At work people bring snacks sometimes, and everyone just eats them standing around the break area, and I never know if what I’m doing looks normal or not. One guy eats like three sandwiches and nobody says anything, so then I’m like, okay, maybe I don’t understand portions.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a time like that. I mostly just remember feeling bad after, more than remembering that it was actually a lot.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe? I’m not totally sure what out of control means there. Like I don’t think I’m blacking out or anything, but sometimes I’ll start eating something and then my head gets loud about it, and I feel like I need to stop, or like I shouldn’t have started. \n\nBut that might just be me being weird about food, not losing control. Like if there’s crackers on the counter, I might have a few and then keep thinking about whether I had too many, and then I’m also thinking about whether my sister left the bag open because she always does that and then they get stale, and it makes me mad because I’m the one who ends up cleaning up the kitchen.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve felt upset or kind of panicky while eating, but I don’t know if I’d call it out of control.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “definitely,” no. Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.\n\nSometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve tried to make up for eating even a small or normal amount of food by not eating for the rest of the day, exercising more than usual, or doing something else to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.\n\nThat’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. So I don’t know if that counts as balancing it out, but yeah, I do avoid eating more after sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or how your body looks has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.\n\nLike before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. And then it’s like that decides the whole day, almost. If I think I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, like I can handle things. But if I think I look bad, it feels like I already messed up somehow.\n\nAnd then I’ll be trying to focus in class, but I’m also thinking about whether my shirt is sitting weird, or if people can tell, which is dumb because everyone’s usually just tired and trying to get through it. But yeah… it affects how I feel about myself more than I want it to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve tried to make up for eating even a small or normal amount of food by not eating for the rest of the day, exercising more than usual, or doing something else to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.\n\nThat’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. So I don’t know if that counts as balancing it out, but yeah, I do avoid eating more after sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or how your body looks has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.\n\nLike before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. And then it’s like that decides the whole day, almost. If I think I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, like I can handle things. But if I think I look bad, it feels like I already messed up somehow.\n\nAnd then I’ll be trying to focus in class, but I’m also thinking about whether my shirt is sitting weird, or if people can tell, which is dumb because everyone’s usually just tired and trying to get through it. But yeah… it affects how I feel about myself more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt that your weight or body shape affected how you judged yourself, even if you logically knew that other things about you were going well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Like, sometimes after I eat something that feels like too much, I get really antsy and I don’t want to just sit there, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt work I’m already moving around a lot anyway, packing boxes and walking back and forth, and sometimes I tell myself that counts. But, um… I don’t know about the other stuff. I don’t really think of it like a plan or anything. It’s more like I just feel uncomfortable and then I try to get through the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself eating a lot in a short amount of time, more than most people would in similar circumstances, and then felt like you needed to do something to make up for it, like overexercising or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… like, “a lot” is hard for me to say. I don’t think I sit down and eat, like, a whole bunch all at once the way people talk about it.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired after the warehouse, I’ll pick at stuff or eat more than I meant to, but then I start thinking about it and get kind of uncomfortable. But I don’t know if that counts. And then I might just try to be careful later, or keep busy, but it’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not something I have a clear name for.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering it right. I just get mixed up when it’s worded like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a lot more than usual in a short amount of time, maybe even to the point of feeling uncomfortable? Did that happen more than once?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I can pick out a time like that exactly. Like sometimes after work, if I haven’t really eaten much, I’ll get home and kind of stand in the kitchen and take bites of random things, and then I’m like, “Why did I do that?” But I don’t know if it’s a lot compared to other people.\n\nFeeling uncomfortable, yeah, but sometimes that’s just because I’m thinking about it too much, not because I’m actually full, if that makes sense. Like my sister can eat a normal plate and just go watch TV, and I’m still thinking about what was on the plate. She also leaves dishes everywhere, which drives me crazy, because then I end up cleaning after her, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nSo… I don’t know. More than once, maybe feeling bothered after eating, but I’m not sure about “a lot in a short time.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a definitely larger amount of food than most people would in a similar situation, like finishing a whole big meal or a lot of snacks in a short time, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I can’t think of a time where I’d say definitely, like more than most people. That word makes me nervous because I don’t really know what most people eat.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I eat something and then keep grabbing little things, like crackers or whatever’s around, and then I feel weird about it, but it’s not like I finished some huge meal. At work people bring snacks sometimes, and everyone just eats them standing around the break area, and I never know if what I’m doing looks normal or not. One guy eats like three sandwiches and nobody says anything, so then I’m like, okay, maybe I don’t understand portions.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a time like that. I mostly just remember feeling bad after, more than remembering that it was actually a lot.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe? I’m not totally sure what out of control means there. Like I don’t think I’m blacking out or anything, but sometimes I’ll start eating something and then my head gets loud about it, and I feel like I need to stop, or like I shouldn’t have started. \n\nBut that might just be me being weird about food, not losing control. Like if there’s crackers on the counter, I might have a few and then keep thinking about whether I had too many, and then I’m also thinking about whether my sister left the bag open because she always does that and then they get stale, and it makes me mad because I’m the one who ends up cleaning up the kitchen.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve felt upset or kind of panicky while eating, but I don’t know if I’d call it out of control.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “definitely,” no. Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.\n\nSometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve tried to make up for eating even a small or normal amount of food by not eating for the rest of the day, exercising more than usual, or doing something else to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.\n\nThat’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. So I don’t know if that counts as balancing it out, but yeah, I do avoid eating more after sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or how your body looks has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.\n\nLike before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. And then it’s like that decides the whole day, almost. If I think I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, like I can handle things. But if I think I look bad, it feels like I already messed up somehow.\n\nAnd then I’ll be trying to focus in class, but I’m also thinking about whether my shirt is sitting weird, or if people can tell, which is dumb because everyone’s usually just tired and trying to get through it. But yeah… it affects how I feel about myself more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt that your weight or body shape affected how you judged yourself, even if you logically knew that other things about you were going well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Like, even if other stuff is technically fine, I’ll look in the mirror before class or before softball and my stomach or my thighs just look wrong, like bigger than they should.\n\nAnd then it kind of takes over. Like I could have gotten myself there on time or handled something okay, but if I think I look bad, it feels like I’m failing at the whole day. If I think I look okay, then I feel like I’m doing good, which I know doesn’t really make sense. But it feels that way in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not like definitely larger than what most people would eat. I mean, I get confused with what’s “normal,” because at the warehouse people will eat these big lunches, like sandwiches and chips and all that, and I’m just kind of sitting there with coffee or whatever, so my idea of a lot is probably off.\n\nBut no, I can’t really think of a time where I ate a huge amount really fast or anything. Sometimes something feels like too much to me, but I don’t know that other people would see it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

The patient continues to endorse restrictive eating patterns, fear of weight gain, and body image concerns, supporting ongoing consideration of F50.00 (Anorexia nervosa, restricting type) and F50.01 (Anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type).",
  "is_final": false

But it feels that way in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not like definitely larger than what most people would eat. I mean, I get confused with what’s “normal,” because at the warehouse people will eat these big lunches, like sandwiches and chips and all that, and I’m just kind of sitting there with coffee or whatever, so my idea of a lot is probably off.\n\nBut no, I can’t really think of a time where I ate a huge amount really fast or anything. Sometimes something feels like too much to me, but I don’t know that other people would see it that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 26 / packer_or_packager / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I don’t really have a clear example of that. Like I said, my idea of “a lot” is probably kind of messed up, because I’ll see people at work eating lunch like it’s nothing, and to me it looks huge, but that doesn’t mean it actually is.

I can think of times where I ate more than I meant to, and then I got stuck thinking about it, but I don’t know if most people would call it a really large amount. Not like some giant amount in a short time. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because the clinic’s been kind of on me about how little I’ve been eating. I mean, I don’t really see it like that all the time, but most days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class.\n\nIt’s just, sitting down for a real meal feels like it messes up everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know? Like my whole day gets thrown off. And the warehouse schedule is already a lot—packing stuff all day, people rushing around—so I just kind of keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not eating much or skipping meals, is it because you’re worried about gaining weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s part of it. Like, if I eat something heavy, like fried food from the break room, or if I’m at church and someone gives me a big plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away.\n\nAnd then it kind of sticks in my head. Like I’ll be trying to do other stuff, folding laundry or thinking about class, and I’m still wondering if I need to check the scale. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But yeah, I do get scared about it.\n\nDoctor: After you eat something that worries you about gaining weight, do you ever try to compensate by doing things like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Not really sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes after I eat something that feels like too much, I get really antsy and I don’t want to just sit there, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt work I’m already moving around a lot anyway, packing boxes and walking back and forth, and sometimes I tell myself that counts. But, um… I don’t know about the other stuff. I don’t really think of it like a plan or anything. It’s more like I just feel uncomfortable and then I try to get through the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself eating a lot in a short amount of time, more than most people would in similar circumstances, and then felt like you needed to do something to make up for it, like overexercising or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… like, “a lot” is hard for me to say. I don’t think I sit down and eat, like, a whole bunch all at once the way people talk about it.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired after the warehouse, I’ll pick at stuff or eat more than I meant to, but then I start thinking about it and get kind of uncomfortable. But I don’t know if that counts. And then I might just try to be careful later, or keep busy, but it’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not something I have a clear name for.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering it right. I just get mixed up when it’s worded like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a lot more than usual in a short amount of time, maybe even to the point of feeling uncomfortable? Did that happen more than once?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I can pick out a time like that exactly. Like sometimes after work, if I haven’t really eaten much, I’ll get home and kind of stand in the kitchen and take bites of random things, and then I’m like, “Why did I do that?” But I don’t know if it’s a lot compared to other people.\n\nFeeling uncomfortable, yeah, but sometimes that’s just because I’m thinking about it too much, not because I’m actually full, if that makes sense. Like my sister can eat a normal plate and just go watch TV, and I’m still thinking about what was on the plate. She also leaves dishes everywhere, which drives me crazy, because then I end up cleaning after her, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nSo… I don’t know. More than once, maybe feeling bothered after eating, but I’m not sure about “a lot in a short time.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a definitely larger amount of food than most people would in a similar situation, like finishing a whole big meal or a lot of snacks in a short time, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I can’t think of a time where I’d say definitely, like more than most people. That word makes me nervous because I don’t really know what most people eat.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I eat something and then keep grabbing little things, like crackers or whatever’s around, and then I feel weird about it, but it’s not like I finished some huge meal. At work people bring snacks sometimes, and everyone just eats them standing around the break area, and I never know if what I’m doing looks normal or not. One guy eats like three sandwiches and nobody says anything, so then I’m like, okay, maybe I don’t understand portions.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a time like that. I mostly just remember feeling bad after, more than remembering that it was actually a lot.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe? I’m not totally sure what out of control means there. Like I don’t think I’m blacking out or anything, but sometimes I’ll start eating something and then my head gets loud about it, and I feel like I need to stop, or like I shouldn’t have started. \n\nBut that might just be me being weird about food, not losing control. Like if there’s crackers on the counter, I might have a few and then keep thinking about whether I had too many, and then I’m also thinking about whether my sister left the bag open because she always does that and then they get stale, and it makes me mad because I’m the one who ends up cleaning up the kitchen.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve felt upset or kind of panicky while eating, but I don’t know if I’d call it out of control.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “definitely,” no. Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.\n\nSometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve tried to make up for eating even a small or normal amount of food by not eating for the rest of the day, exercising more than usual, or doing something else to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.\n\nThat’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. So I don’t know if that counts as balancing it out, but yeah, I do avoid eating more after sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or how your body looks has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.\n\nLike before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. I’m not sure… I don’t really think of it like I can’t stop. It’s more like, if I eat something I wasn’t planning to, I’ll get really stuck on it after, but during it I don’t know if I’d say I’m out of control.

Like my whole day gets thrown off. And the warehouse schedule is already a lot—packing stuff all day, people rushing around—so I just kind of keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not eating much or skipping meals, is it because you’re worried about gaining weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s part of it. Like, if I eat something heavy, like fried food from the break room, or if I’m at church and someone gives me a big plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away.\n\nAnd then it kind of sticks in my head. Like I’ll be trying to do other stuff, folding laundry or thinking about class, and I’m still wondering if I need to check the scale. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But yeah, I do get scared about it.\n\nDoctor: After you eat something that worries you about gaining weight, do you ever try to compensate by doing things like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Not really sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes after I eat something that feels like too much, I get really antsy and I don’t want to just sit there, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt work I’m already moving around a lot anyway, packing boxes and walking back and forth, and sometimes I tell myself that counts. But, um… I don’t know about the other stuff. I don’t really think of it like a plan or anything. It’s more like I just feel uncomfortable and then I try to get through the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself eating a lot in a short amount of time, more than most people would in similar circumstances, and then felt like you needed to do something to make up for it, like overexercising or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… like, “a lot” is hard for me to say. I don’t think I sit down and eat, like, a whole bunch all at once the way people talk about it.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired after the warehouse, I’ll pick at stuff or eat more than I meant to, but then I start thinking about it and get kind of uncomfortable. But I don’t know if that counts. And then I might just try to be careful later, or keep busy, but it’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not something I have a clear name for.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering it right. I just get mixed up when it’s worded like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a lot more than usual in a short amount of time, maybe even to the point of feeling uncomfortable? Did that happen more than once?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I can pick out a time like that exactly. Like sometimes after work, if I haven’t really eaten much, I’ll get home and kind of stand in the kitchen and take bites of random things, and then I’m like, “Why did I do that?” But I don’t know if it’s a lot compared to other people.\n\nFeeling uncomfortable, yeah, but sometimes that’s just because I’m thinking about it too much, not because I’m actually full, if that makes sense. Like my sister can eat a normal plate and just go watch TV, and I’m still thinking about what was on the plate. She also leaves dishes everywhere, which drives me crazy, because then I end up cleaning after her, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nSo… I don’t know. More than once, maybe feeling bothered after eating, but I’m not sure about “a lot in a short time.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a definitely larger amount of food than most people would in a similar situation, like finishing a whole big meal or a lot of snacks in a short time, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I can’t think of a time where I’d say definitely, like more than most people. That word makes me nervous because I don’t really know what most people eat.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I eat something and then keep grabbing little things, like crackers or whatever’s around, and then I feel weird about it, but it’s not like I finished some huge meal. At work people bring snacks sometimes, and everyone just eats them standing around the break area, and I never know if what I’m doing looks normal or not. One guy eats like three sandwiches and nobody says anything, so then I’m like, okay, maybe I don’t understand portions.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a time like that. I mostly just remember feeling bad after, more than remembering that it was actually a lot.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe? I’m not totally sure what out of control means there. Like I don’t think I’m blacking out or anything, but sometimes I’ll start eating something and then my head gets loud about it, and I feel like I need to stop, or like I shouldn’t have started. \n\nBut that might just be me being weird about food, not losing control. Like if there’s crackers on the counter, I might have a few and then keep thinking about whether I had too many, and then I’m also thinking about whether my sister left the bag open because she always does that and then they get stale, and it makes me mad because I’m the one who ends up cleaning up the kitchen.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve felt upset or kind of panicky while eating, but I don’t know if I’d call it out of control.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “definitely,” no. Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.\n\nSometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve tried to make up for eating even a small or normal amount of food by not eating for the rest of the day, exercising more than usual, or doing something else to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.\n\nThat’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. So I don’t know if that counts as balancing it out, but yeah, I do avoid eating more after sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or how your body looks has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.\n\nLike before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. And then it’s like that decides the whole day, almost. If I think I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, like I can handle things. But if I think I look bad, it feels like I already messed up somehow.\n\nAnd then I’ll be trying to focus in class, but I’m also thinking about whether my shirt is sitting weird, or if people can tell, which is dumb because everyone’s usually just tired and trying to get through it. But yeah… it affects how I feel about myself more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt that your weight or body shape affected how you judged yourself, even if you logically knew that other things about you were going well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. I’ve felt upset or kind of panicky while eating, but I don’t know if I’d call it out of control.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “definitely,” no. Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.\n\nSometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve tried to make up for eating even a small or normal amount of food by not eating for the rest of the day, exercising more than usual, or doing something else to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.\n\nThat’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. So I don’t know if that counts as balancing it out, but yeah, I do avoid eating more after sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or how your body looks has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.\n\nLike before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. And then it’s like that decides the whole day, almost. If I think I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, like I can handle things. But if I think I look bad, it feels like I already messed up somehow.\n\nAnd then I’ll be trying to focus in class, but I’m also thinking about whether my shirt is sitting weird, or if people can tell, which is dumb because everyone’s usually just tired and trying to get through it. But yeah… it affects how I feel about myself more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt that your weight or body shape affected how you judged yourself, even if you logically knew that other things about you were going well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Like, even if other stuff is technically fine, I’ll look in the mirror before class or before softball and my stomach or my thighs just look wrong, like bigger than they should.\n\nAnd then it kind of takes over. Like I could have gotten myself there on time or handled something okay, but if I think I look bad, it feels like I’m failing at the whole day. If I think I look okay, then I feel like I’m doing good, which I know doesn’t really make sense. But it feels that way in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not like definitely larger than what most people would eat. I mean, I get confused with what’s “normal,” because at the warehouse people will eat these big lunches, like sandwiches and chips and all that, and I’m just kind of sitting there with coffee or whatever, so my idea of a lot is probably off.\n\nBut no, I can’t really think of a time where I ate a huge amount really fast or anything. Sometimes something feels like too much to me, but I don’t know that other people would see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really have a clear example of that. Like I said, my idea of “a lot” is probably kind of messed up, because I’ll see people at work eating lunch like it’s nothing, and to me it looks huge, but that doesn’t mean it actually is.\n\nI can think of times where I ate more than I meant to, and then I got stuck thinking about it, but I don’t know if most people would call it a really large amount. Not like some giant amount in a short time. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, where you felt like you couldn’t stop eating or couldn’t control what or how much you were eating, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really think of it like I can’t stop. It’s more like, if I eat something I wasn’t planning to, I’ll get really stuck on it after, but during it I don’t know if I’d say I’m out of control.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be at work and someone brings in snacks, and everyone’s just standing around talking, and I’ll be thinking about whether I should take one, but then I’m also thinking about how I have to finish packing this one order before the line backs up, and then—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear time where I felt like I couldn’t control what or how much I was eating. I don’t think so… not the way you’re describing it, like a big meal or a lot of snacks and feeling like I couldn’t stop.

I mean, there are times I eat when I’m not really hungry, like if something’s just there, but then I usually start thinking about it right away and I don’t really keep going. So… no, I can’t think of a time where it was a large amount in a short time and I couldn’t stop myself. Not really.

Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.\n\nSometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.\n\nLike before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. And then it’s like that decides the whole day, almost. If I think I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, like I can handle things. But if I think I look bad, it feels like I already messed up somehow.\n\nAnd then I’ll be trying to focus in class, but I’m also thinking about whether my shirt is sitting weird, or if people can tell, which is dumb because everyone’s usually just tired and trying to get through it. But yeah… it affects how I feel about myself more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt that your weight or body shape affected how you judged yourself, even if you logically knew that other things about you were going well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Like, even if other stuff is technically fine, I’ll look in the mirror before class or before softball and my stomach or my thighs just look wrong, like bigger than they should.\n\nAnd then it kind of takes over. Like I could have gotten myself there on time or handled something okay, but if I think I look bad, it feels like I’m failing at the whole day. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… I know you’ve asked it a couple ways, and I’m trying to think of something that fits, but nothing really comes to mind.

Like, I’ve eaten when I wasn’t hungry, sure, but not “a lot” and not where I felt like I couldn’t stop myself. Usually I’m thinking too much to even get to that point. I’ll be like, okay, did I already eat earlier, what am I supposed to do after work, and then I start thinking about whether I left my box cutter on the table or if my supervisor saw me standing around—sorry.

But no, I don’t really have a time like that. Not that I can think of.

I mean, I don’t really see it like that all the time, but most days I just grab coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class.\n\nIt’s just, sitting down for a real meal feels like it messes up everything I’m trying to keep in line, you know? Like my whole day gets thrown off. And the warehouse schedule is already a lot—packing stuff all day, people rushing around—so I just kind of keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you think about not eating much or skipping meals, is it because you’re worried about gaining weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s part of it. Like, if I eat something heavy, like fried food from the break room, or if I’m at church and someone gives me a big plate, I start thinking it’s going to show on me right away.\n\nAnd then it kind of sticks in my head. Like I’ll be trying to do other stuff, folding laundry or thinking about class, and I’m still wondering if I need to check the scale. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But yeah, I do get scared about it.\n\nDoctor: After you eat something that worries you about gaining weight, do you ever try to compensate by doing things like exercising a lot, making yourself vomit, or using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Not really sure how to answer that. Like, sometimes after I eat something that feels like too much, I get really antsy and I don’t want to just sit there, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nAt work I’m already moving around a lot anyway, packing boxes and walking back and forth, and sometimes I tell myself that counts. But, um… I don’t know about the other stuff. I don’t really think of it like a plan or anything. It’s more like I just feel uncomfortable and then I try to get through the rest of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself eating a lot in a short amount of time, more than most people would in similar circumstances, and then felt like you needed to do something to make up for it, like overexercising or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… like, “a lot” is hard for me to say. I don’t think I sit down and eat, like, a whole bunch all at once the way people talk about it.\n\nSometimes if I’m really tired after the warehouse, I’ll pick at stuff or eat more than I meant to, but then I start thinking about it and get kind of uncomfortable. But I don’t know if that counts. And then I might just try to be careful later, or keep busy, but it’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not something I have a clear name for.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering it right. I just get mixed up when it’s worded like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a lot more than usual in a short amount of time, maybe even to the point of feeling uncomfortable? Did that happen more than once?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I don’t know if I can pick out a time like that exactly. Like sometimes after work, if I haven’t really eaten much, I’ll get home and kind of stand in the kitchen and take bites of random things, and then I’m like, “Why did I do that?” But I don’t know if it’s a lot compared to other people.\n\nFeeling uncomfortable, yeah, but sometimes that’s just because I’m thinking about it too much, not because I’m actually full, if that makes sense. Like my sister can eat a normal plate and just go watch TV, and I’m still thinking about what was on the plate. She also leaves dishes everywhere, which drives me crazy, because then I end up cleaning after her, and then I forget what I was doing.\n\nSo… I don’t know. More than once, maybe feeling bothered after eating, but I’m not sure about “a lot in a short time.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt like you ate a definitely larger amount of food than most people would in a similar situation, like finishing a whole big meal or a lot of snacks in a short time, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I can’t think of a time where I’d say definitely, like more than most people. That word makes me nervous because I don’t really know what most people eat.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I eat something and then keep grabbing little things, like crackers or whatever’s around, and then I feel weird about it, but it’s not like I finished some huge meal. At work people bring snacks sometimes, and everyone just eats them standing around the break area, and I never know if what I’m doing looks normal or not. One guy eats like three sandwiches and nobody says anything, so then I’m like, okay, maybe I don’t understand portions.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really point to a time like that. I mostly just remember feeling bad after, more than remembering that it was actually a lot.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe? I’m not totally sure what out of control means there. Like I don’t think I’m blacking out or anything, but sometimes I’ll start eating something and then my head gets loud about it, and I feel like I need to stop, or like I shouldn’t have started. \n\nBut that might just be me being weird about food, not losing control. Like if there’s crackers on the counter, I might have a few and then keep thinking about whether I had too many, and then I’m also thinking about whether my sister left the bag open because she always does that and then they get stale, and it makes me mad because I’m the one who ends up cleaning up the kitchen.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’ve felt upset or kind of panicky while eating, but I don’t know if I’d call it out of control.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say “definitely,” no. Like, I know I’ve felt bad after eating, or like I shouldn’t have had something, but if you’re asking whether it was more than most people would eat in that same time… I don’t really know.\n\nSometimes I compare myself to people at work, but then everyone eats so differently. Someone will have a whole fast food meal and chips and soda, and I’m over there thinking about one thing I ate, so my sense of it is probably not normal. And then I start thinking about whether people noticed, which is stupid because they’re probably just trying to get through the shift.\n\nSo… no, I can’t really think of a time I’d honestly call definitely larger than what most people would eat.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you’ve tried to make up for eating even a small or normal amount of food by not eating for the rest of the day, exercising more than usual, or doing something else to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I think of it like “making up for it,” exactly. It’s more like if I eat, even something small, then the rest of the day gets kind of… messed up in my head, like I need to keep everything lined up.\n\nThat’s part of why they’ve been on me at the clinic, because I guess I’m not eating much. Most days I just have coffee before I go to the warehouse, and then maybe later I’ll have a yogurt or a few bites of something after GED class. Sitting down for a real meal feels like it throws everything off, and then I’m thinking about it the whole time instead of just doing what I need to do. So I don’t know if that counts as balancing it out, but yeah, I do avoid eating more after sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your weight or how your body looks has a strong impact on how you feel about yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does. I don’t really like saying that, because it sounds shallow or something, but it’s not like I’m trying to be that way.\n\nLike before GED class, or before softball, I’ll look in the mirror and my stomach or my thighs just look… bigger than they should. And then it’s like that decides the whole day, almost. If I think I look okay, I feel like I’m doing good, like I can handle things. But if I think I look bad, it feels like I already messed up somehow.\n\nAnd then I’ll be trying to focus in class, but I’m also thinking about whether my shirt is sitting weird, or if people can tell, which is dumb because everyone’s usually just tired and trying to get through it. But yeah… it affects how I feel about myself more than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt that your weight or body shape affected how you judged yourself, even if you logically knew that other things about you were going well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Like, even if other stuff is technically fine, I’ll look in the mirror before class or before softball and my stomach or my thighs just look wrong, like bigger than they should.\n\nAnd then it kind of takes over. Like I could have gotten myself there on time or handled something okay, but if I think I look bad, it feels like I’m failing at the whole day. If I think I look okay, then I feel like I’m doing good, which I know doesn’t really make sense. But it feels that way in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not like definitely larger than what most people would eat. I mean, I get confused with what’s “normal,” because at the warehouse people will eat these big lunches, like sandwiches and chips and all that, and I’m just kind of sitting there with coffee or whatever, so my idea of a lot is probably off.\n\nBut no, I can’t really think of a time where I ate a huge amount really fast or anything. Sometimes something feels like too much to me, but I don’t know that other people would see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really have a clear example of that. Like I said, my idea of “a lot” is probably kind of messed up, because I’ll see people at work eating lunch like it’s nothing, and to me it looks huge, but that doesn’t mean it actually is.\n\nI can think of times where I ate more than I meant to, and then I got stuck thinking about it, but I don’t know if most people would call it a really large amount. Not like some giant amount in a short time. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, where you felt like you couldn’t stop eating or couldn’t control what or how much you were eating, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really think of it like I can’t stop. It’s more like, if I eat something I wasn’t planning to, I’ll get really stuck on it after, but during it I don’t know if I’d say I’m out of control.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be at work and someone brings in snacks, and everyone’s just standing around talking, and I’ll be thinking about whether I should take one, but then I’m also thinking about how I have to finish packing this one order before the line backs up, and then—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear time where I felt like I couldn’t control what or how much I was eating. It’s more that I worry about it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time—like a big meal or a lot of snacks—when you weren’t really hungry, and felt like you couldn’t stop yourself, even if just for a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not the way you’re describing it, like a big meal or a lot of snacks and feeling like I couldn’t stop.\n\nI mean, there are times I eat when I’m not really hungry, like if something’s just there, but then I usually start thinking about it right away and I don’t really keep going. Like at work, if there’s donuts or something in the break room, I’ll stand there way too long thinking about whether to take one, and then someone will come in and start talking about the schedule or the scanner acting up, and then I’m distracted and I don’t even know why I was standing there.\n\nSo… no, I can’t think of a time where it was a large amount in a short time and I couldn’t stop myself. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you've eaten a lot of food in a short time, even when you weren't hungry, and felt unable to stop yourself, even if just for a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I know you’ve asked it a couple ways, and I’m trying to think of something that fits, but nothing really comes to mind.\n\nLike, I’ve eaten when I wasn’t hungry, sure, but not “a lot” and not where I felt like I couldn’t stop myself. Sometimes something feels like too much to me, but I don’t know that other people would see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when you ate an amount of food that you yourself would say is definitely larger than what most people would eat in the same amount of time, even if you weren’t really hungry?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really have a clear example of that. Like I said, my idea of “a lot” is probably kind of messed up, because I’ll see people at work eating lunch like it’s nothing, and to me it looks huge, but that doesn’t mean it actually is.\n\nI can think of times where I ate more than I meant to, and then I got stuck thinking about it, but I don’t know if most people would call it a really large amount. Not like some giant amount in a short time. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt you were eating in a way that was out of your control, where you felt like you couldn’t stop eating or couldn’t control what or how much you were eating, even if it wasn’t a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really think of it like I can’t stop. It’s more like, if I eat something I wasn’t planning to, I’ll get really stuck on it after, but during it I don’t know if I’d say I’m out of control.\n\nLike sometimes I’ll be at work and someone brings in snacks, and everyone’s just standing around talking, and I’ll be thinking about whether I should take one, but then I’m also thinking about how I have to finish packing this one order before the line backs up, and then—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear time where I felt like I couldn’t control what or how much I was eating. It’s more that I worry about it a lot.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you ate a large amount of food in a short period of time—like a big meal or a lot of snacks—when you weren’t really hungry, and felt like you couldn’t stop yourself, even if just for a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not the way you’re describing it, like a big meal or a lot of snacks and feeling like I couldn’t stop.\n\nI mean, there are times I eat when I’m not really hungry, like if something’s just there, but then I usually start thinking about it right away and I don’t really keep going. Like at work, if there’s donuts or something in the break room, I’ll stand there way too long thinking about whether to take one, and then someone will come in and start talking about the schedule or the scanner acting up, and then I’m distracted and I don’t even know why I was standing there.\n\nSo… no, I can’t think of a time where it was a large amount in a short time and I couldn’t stop myself. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you've eaten a lot of food in a short time, even when you weren't hungry, and felt unable to stop yourself, even if just for a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I know you’ve asked it a couple ways, and I’m trying to think of something that fits, but nothing really comes to mind.\n\nLike, I’ve eaten when I wasn’t hungry, sure, but not “a lot” and not where I felt like I couldn’t stop myself. Usually I’m thinking too much to even get to that point. I’ll be like, okay, did I already eat earlier, what am I supposed to do after work, and then I start thinking about whether I left my box cutter on the table or if my supervisor saw me standing around—sorry.\n\nBut no, I don’t really have a time like that. Not that I can think of."
